I've uploaded a python code repository in the directory tobymao__sqlglot__25.31. Consider the following issue description:

<issue_description>
User defined function recognized as column (Snowflake dialect)
This query:
`select COL1,COL2 from some_table,TABLE(SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1))`
`p = sqlglot.parse_one(query, dialect=sqlglot.Dialects.SNOWFLAKE)`
creates this ast:
`Select(
  expressions=[
    Column(
      this=Identifier(this=COL1, quoted=False)),
    Column(
      this=Identifier(this=COL2, quoted=False))],
  from=From(
    this=Table(
      this=Identifier(this=some_table, quoted=False))),
  joins=[
    Join(
      this=Table(
        this=Anonymous(
          this=TABLE,
          expressions=[
            Column(
              this=Window(
                this=Anonymous(
                  this=TABLE_FUNC,
                  expressions=[
                    Column(
                      this=Identifier(this=value1, quoted=False)),
                    Column(
                      this=Identifier(this=value2, quoted=False))]),
                partition_by=[
                  Column(
                    this=Identifier(this=value1, quoted=False))],
                over=OVER),
              table=Identifier(this=SOME_SCHEMA, quoted=False),
              db=Identifier(this=SOME_DB, quoted=False))])))])`

Inside the anonymous TABLE (which is also a function that returns values as a table you can select on) we can see the expressions contain only one expression which should be another function but is recognized as a Column.

Verified this still happens on 25.31.4.
dialect: Snowflake
</issue_description>

Let me start by understanding the problem.

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me carefully read and understand the issue:

1. The problem is with parsing SQL queries in Snowflake dialect
2. Specifically, when parsing a query with TABLE() function that contains another function with OVER clause
3. The issue is that SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) inside TABLE() is being parsed incorrectly
4. Instead of recognizing TABLE_FUNC as a function, it's being recognized as a Column
5. This affects the AST generation where expressions inside the Anonymous TABLE should contain a function but contains a Column instead

Key technical details:
- Query: `select COL1,COL2 from some_table,TABLE(SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1))`

### 1.1 Problem Analysis
The issue involves incorrect parsing of Snowflake SQL dialect where user-defined table functions inside `TABLE()` constructs are misidentified as column references instead of function calls.

### 1.2 Technical Details
- **Method/Function**: `sqlglot.parse_one()` with `dialect=sqlglot.Dialects.SNOWFLAKE`
- **Query pattern**: `TABLE(SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1))`
- **Error**: `TABLE_FUNC` is parsed as `Column` instead of `Anonymous` function
- **Expected AST structure**: `Anonymous(TABLE)` should contain `Anonymous(TABLE_FUNC)` as expressions
- **Actual AST structure**: Contains `Column(Window(Anonymous(TABLE_FUNC)))` instead

### 1.3 Clear Problem Statement
In Snowflake dialect parsing, when a user-defined table function with window clause (`OVER`) is wrapped in `TABLE()`, the parser incorrectly treats the inner function as a column reference rather than preserving it as a function call.

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Parse query: `select COL1,COL2 from some_table,TABLE(SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1))`
2. Use `sqlglot.parse_one(query, dialect=sqlglot.Dialects.SNOWFLAKE)`
3. Examine the AST under `joins[0].this.expressions[0]`
4. Observe that `TABLE_FUNC` is wrapped in `Column` instead of being direct `Anonymous` function

### 1.5 Best Practices for Testing and Fixing
- Understand Snowflake's TABLE function syntax and parsing rules
- Make minimal changes to avoid affecting other dialects
- Test both with and without window clauses
- Ensure backward compatibility with existing functionality

     1	![SQLGlot logo](sqlglot.png)
     2	
     3	SQLGlot is a no-dependency SQL parser, transpiler, optimizer, and engine. It can be used to format SQL or translate between [23 different dialects](https://github.com/tobymao/sqlglot/blob/main/sqlglot/dialects/__init__.py) like [DuckDB](https://duckdb.org/), [Presto](https://prestodb.io/) / [Trino](https://trino.io/), [Spark](https://spark.apache.org/) / [Databricks](https://www.databricks.com/), [Snowflake](https://www.snowflake.com/en/), and [BigQuery](https://cloud.google.com/bigquery/). It aims to read a wide variety of SQL inputs and output syntactically and semantically correct SQL in the targeted dialects.
     4	
     5	It is a very comprehensive generic SQL parser with a robust [test suite](https://github.com/tobymao/sqlglot/blob/main/tests/). It is also quite [performant](#benchmarks), while being written purely in Python.
     6	
     7	You can easily [customize](#custom-dialects) the parser, [analyze](#metadata) queries, traverse expression trees, and programmatically [build](#build-and-modify-sql) SQL.
     8	
     9	Syntax [errors](#parser-errors) are highlighted and dialect incompatibilities can warn or raise depending on configurations. However, SQLGlot does not aim to be a SQL validator, so it may fail to detect certain syntax errors.
    10	
    11	Learn more about SQLGlot in the API [documentation](https://sqlglot.com/) and the expression tree [primer](https://github.com/tobymao/sqlglot/blob/main/posts/ast_primer.md).
    12	
    13	Contributions are very welcome in SQLGlot; read the [contribution guide](https://github.com/tobymao/sqlglot/blob/main/CONTRIBUTING.md) and the [onboarding document](https://github.com/tobymao/sqlglot/blob/main/posts/onboarding.md) to get started!

     8	    NormalizationStrategy,
     9	    binary_from_function,
    10	    build_default_decimal_type,
    11	    build_timestamp_from_parts,

    30	from sqlglot.generator import unsupported_args
    31	from sqlglot.helper import flatten, is_float, is_int, seq_get
    32	from sqlglot.tokens import TokenType
    33	
    34	if t.TYPE_CHECKING:
    35	    from sqlglot._typing import E
    36	
    37	
    38	# from https://docs.snowflake.com/en/sql-reference/functions/to_timestamp.html
    39	def _build_datetime(
    40	    name: str, kind: exp.DataType.Type, safe: bool = False
    41	) -> t.Callable[[t.List], exp.Func]:
    42	    def _builder(args: t.List) -> exp.Func:
    43	        value = seq_get(args, 0)
    44	        int_value = value is not None and is_int(value.name)
    45	
    46	        if isinstance(value, exp.Literal):
    47	            # Converts calls like `TO_TIME('01:02:03')` into casts
    48	            if len(args) == 1 and value.is_string and not int_value:

import sqlglot

# The problematic query from the issue

# Parse with Snowflake dialect

print("Generated AST:")
print(p)
print()

# Let's examine the structure more closely
print("Examining joins structure:")
joins = p.args.get('joins', [])
for i, join in enumerate(joins):
    print(f"Join {i}:")
    print(f"  Type: {type(join)}")
    print(f"  This: {join.this}")
    print(f"  This type: {type(join.this)}")
    
    # Look at the expressions inside the TABLE anonymous function
    if hasattr(join.this, 'expressions'):
        print(f"  Expressions: {join.this.expressions}")
        for j, expr in enumerate(join.this.expressions):
            print(f"    Expression {j}: {expr}")
            print(f"    Expression {j} type: {type(expr)}")
            
            # Check if this is the problematic part - the TABLE_FUNC should be Anonymous, not Column
            if hasattr(expr, 'this'):
                print(f"    Expression {j}.this: {expr.this}")
                print(f"    Expression {j}.this type: {type(expr.this)}")
                
                # If it's a Window, check its 'this' attribute
                if hasattr(expr.this, 'this'):
                    print(f"    Expression {j}.this.this: {expr.this.this}")
                    print(f"    Expression {j}.this.this type: {type(expr.this.this)}")
                    
                    # This is where the issue lies - TABLE_FUNC should be Anonymous, not Column
                    if hasattr(expr.this.this, 'expressions'):
                        print(f"    Expression {j}.this.this.expressions: {expr.this.this.expressions}")
                        for k, sub_expr in enumerate(expr.this.this.expressions):
                            print(f"      Sub-expression {k}: {sub_expr}")
                            print(f"      Sub-expression {k} type: {type(sub_expr)}")

print()

1. The `TABLE` function is correctly parsed as `Anonymous(this=TABLE, expressions=[...])`
2. Inside that, we have a `Column` wrapping a `Window` expression
3. The `Window.this` is correctly `Anonymous(this=TABLE_FUNC, expressions=[...])`
4. But the issue is that `TABLE_FUNC` is wrapped in a `Column` instead of being directly in the expressions

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Understanding the Problem<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Looking at the AST structure, I can see the issue clearly now:

1. The query is: `TABLE(SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1))`

2. What SHOULD happen:
   - TABLE should be parsed as Anonymous(this="TABLE", expressions=[Anonymous(this="TABLE_FUNC", ...)])
   - TABLE_FUNC should be parsed as Anonymous function directly

3. What ACTUALLY happens:
   - TABLE is parsed as Anonymous(this="TABLE", expressions=[Column(this=Window(...))])
   - The Window contains the Anonymous TABLE_FUNC correctly, but it's wrapped in Column

The problem seems to be in how the parsing of table expressions works. When parsing the TABLE function argument, it's treating `SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(value1, value2) over (PARTITION BY value1)` as a column reference with a window clause, rather than as a standalone function call.

Looking at the parsing flow:
1. `_parse_table()` is called for the JOIN
2. This eventually calls `_parse_table_parts()` 
3. Which calls `_parse_table_part()` for each part
4. `_parse_table_part()` tries `_parse_function(optional_parens=False)` first
5. This should parse TABLE_FUNC as a function, but somehow it's getting wrapped in Column

The issue is likely in the `_parse_table_part` method or in how the Window parsing interacts with table parsing.

Let me trace this more carefully:

In `_parse_table_part`:
```python
def _parse_table_part(self, schema: bool = False) -> t.Optional[exp.Expression]:
    return (
        (not schema and self._parse_function(optional_parens=False))
        or self._parse_id_var(any_token=False)
        or self._parse_string_as_identifier()
        or self._parse_placeholder()
    )
```

So for `SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(...)`, it should:
1. Parse SOME_DB as a table part (identifier)
2. Parse SOME_SCHEMA as a table part (identifier)  
3. Parse TABLE_FUNC(...) as a function call

But somewhere in this process, when there's an OVER clause, it's getting treated as a column with window function rather than a standalone function.

Looking at the actual AST, I see:
- The outer TABLE is correctly parsed as Anonymous
- But its expression is Column(Window(Anonymous(TABLE_FUNC)))

This suggests the issue is that when parsing the argument to TABLE(), the parser is treating the entire `SOME_DB.SOME_SCHEMA.TABLE_FUNC(...) OVER (...)` as a column expression rather than parsing TABLE_FUNC as a function directly.

The problem is likely in how `_parse_function_call` interacts with window parsing when it's being called in the context of table parsing.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me look at how the TABLE function arguments are parsed. 1. **Without OVER clause**: `TABLE(SOME_FUNC())` → `Anonymous(SOME_FUNC)` ✓ (correct)

Let me look at how table parsing works when there are dots involved. The key insight is that when parsing `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER (...)`, the parser sees:
- `DB` as database
- `SCHEMA` as schema 
- `FUNC() OVER (...)` as a column with window function

But what should happen is:
- `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` should be parsed as a function call
- `OVER (...)` should be part of that function call

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>The issue is that `_parse_table_part` tries to parse a function first, but only when `schema=False`. When parsing table parts in the context of a table (not schema), it should be able to parse functions.

However, the problem arises when we have `FUNC() OVER (...)`. The `_parse_function` method correctly parses `FUNC()` as an Anonymous function, but then `_parse_window` wraps it in a Window expression. And somehow this whole thing gets wrapped in a Column.

Let me trace through what happens when parsing `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER (...)`:

1. Parser sees `DB` - calls `_parse_table_part()` → returns Identifier(DB)
2. Parser sees `.` - continues
3. Parser sees `SCHEMA` - calls `_parse_table_part()` → returns Identifier(SCHEMA)  
4. Parser sees `.` - continues
5. Parser sees `FUNC` - calls `_parse_table_part()` → calls `_parse_function()` → returns Anonymous(FUNC)
6. Parser continues and sees `OVER (...)` - this triggers window parsing

But somewhere in this process, the Window gets wrapped in a Column.

Let me look at how the Column wrapping happens. I suspect it's in the `_parse_column` or related methods.

Actually, let me step back and look at how the TABLE function itself is parsed. The TABLE function is parsed correctly, but its arguments are not.

Looking at the debug output, when we have `TABLE(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ())`, the TABLE function gets:
`expressions=[Column(this=Window(this=Anonymous(this=FUNC), over=OVER), table=Identifier(SCHEMA), db=Identifier(DB))]`

This looks like the parser is treating `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()` as a qualified column reference with window function, rather than as a function call.

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see exactly what's happening:

**Without OVER clause**: `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` → `Dot(Dot(Identifier(DB), Identifier(SCHEMA)), Anonymous(FUNC))` ✓

**With OVER clause**: `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()` → `Column(Window(Anonymous(FUNC)), table=SCHEMA, db=DB)` ✗

So the issue is that when there's an OVER clause, the parser treats `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a qualified column reference instead of a function call, and then wraps the Window in a Column.

This is happening because in the parsing logic, when the parser encounters `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()`, it:
1. Parses `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a qualified column (because of the dots)
2. Then sees `OVER ()` and applies window parsing to that column
3. The result is `Column(Window(...), table=SCHEMA, db=DB)`

But what SHOULD happen is:
1. Parse `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a function call 
2. Apply window parsing to that function
3. The result should be `Window(Anonymous(FUNC), ...)` without the Column wrapper

The problem is in the precedence/order of parsing. When parsing fields/columns, the parser prioritizes qualified column references over function calls when there are dots involved.

Let me look at where this decision is made. It's likely in the `_parse_field` or `_parse_column_reference` methods.

Looking back at the parsing flow:
1. When parsing the TABLE function arguments, it calls some parsing method
2. That method eventually calls `_parse_field` or similar
3. `_parse_field` tries `_parse_function` but if there are dots, it might treat it as a column

Actually, let me check how the TABLE function arguments are parsed. The TABLE function itself is parsed correctly, but its arguments are not.

The issue is conceptual: in SQL, when we have `TABLE(function_call OVER (...))`, the `function_call` should be parsed as a function, not as a column reference.

But the current parser logic treats `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a potential qualified column reference when there are dots, and only falls back to function parsing in certain contexts.

The fix should ensure that when parsing function arguments (especially for TABLE functions), function calls are prioritized over qualified column references.

Let me look at the exact place where this parsing happens. When we parse `TABLE(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ())`, the argument `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()` is parsed by `_parse_lambda` → `_parse_select_or_expression` → `_parse_assignment` → `_parse_disjunction` → ... 

Eventually this leads to parsing that treats `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a column.

But looking at the issue more carefully, I think the real problem might be earlier in the parsing chain.

Actually, let me step back and think about the correct approach.

```
Anonymous(
  this=TABLE,
  expressions=[
    Window(
      this=Anonymous(
        this=TABLE_FUNC,
        expressions=[...]
      ),
      partition_by=[...],
      over=OVER
    )
  ]
)
```

vs what we GET:
```
Anonymous(
  this=TABLE,
  expressions=[
    Column(
      this=Window(
        this=Anonymous(
          this=TABLE_FUNC,
          expressions=[...]
        ),
        partition_by=[...],
        over=OVER
      ),
      table=Identifier(SCHEMA),
      db=Identifier(DB)
    )
  ]
)
```

The difference is that the Window should be a direct expression, not wrapped in a Column with db/table qualifiers.

So the fix should be to prevent the wrapping of Window expressions in Columns when they're function calls.

Let me look at where Columns get their db/table qualifiers. Looking at the expressions, the Column has `table=Identifier(SCHEMA), db=Identifier(DB)`. This suggests that the parser is interpreting `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a qualified column reference `DB.SCHEMA.(FUNC() OVER (...))` rather than as `(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()) OVER (...)`.

Wait, that's not right either. Looking more carefully:

The Column has:
- `this=Window(...)` 
- `table=Identifier(SCHEMA)`
- `db=Identifier(DB)`

This means the parser interpreted it as: column named `(FUNC() OVER (...))` in schema `SCHEMA` and database `DB`.

But what we want is: function `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` with window clause.

I think the issue is in the order of parsing. The parser should recognize `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a function call BEFORE trying to treat it as a qualified column.

Let me check if there's a way to modify the parsing precedence.

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement

Based on my investigation, here's the clear problem:

**Problem**: When parsing `TABLE(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER (...))` in Snowflake dialect, the parser treats `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as a qualified column reference instead of a function call, resulting in `Column(Window(Anonymous(FUNC), ...), table=SCHEMA, db=DB)` instead of `Window(Anonymous(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC), ...)`.

**Location**: The issue is in the parsing precedence in `sqlglot/parser.py`. When parsing expressions that could be either qualified column references or function calls with dots, the parser defaults to column reference interpretation.

**How the test reproduces the issue**: The reproduction script shows that `TABLE(DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ())` produces a Column-wrapped Window instead of a direct Window.

**Best practices for the fix**: 
1. Make minimal changes to avoid breaking existing functionality
2. Ensure the fix is specific to the TABLE function context or window function context
3. Preserve backward compatibility for regular column references

**How to fix**: The fix should modify the parsing logic to prioritize function calls over qualified column references when parsing function arguments, especially in contexts where window clauses may follow.

## Phase 6. FIX IMPLEMENTATION

Looking at the issue more carefully, I believe the problem is in how qualified names with function calls are parsed. When we have `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER (...)`, the parser should recognize `FUNC()` as a function call first, then apply the qualification and window parsing.

   742

   744	            # can be joined in a query with a comma separator, as well as closing paren
   745	            # in case of subqueries

        def _parse_table_function(self) -> t.Optional[exp.Expression]:
            """Parse TABLE function arguments with priority for function calls over qualified columns."""
            # Parse the TABLE function call normally first
            if not self._match(TokenType.L_PAREN):
                return None
            
            # Parse arguments with special handling for potential function calls with OVER clauses
            args = self._parse_csv(self._parse_table_function_arg)
            
            if not self._match(TokenType.R_PAREN):
                return None
                
            # Create the TABLE function expression
            table_func = self.expression(exp.Anonymous, this="TABLE", expressions=args or [])
            
            # Apply window parsing if needed
            return self._parse_window(table_func)

        def _parse_table_function_arg(self) -> t.Optional[exp.Expression]:
            """Parse TABLE function arguments with preference for function calls."""
            # Save current position for backtracking
            index = self._index
            
            # Try parsing as a potential function call first (with anonymous_func=True)
            arg = self._parse_field(any_token=True, anonymous_func=True)
            
            # If we couldn't parse anything, fall back to standard parsing
            if not arg:
                self._retreat(index)
                return self._parse_assignment()
                
            return arg

The problem is that `_parse_window` is called AFTER the closing parenthesis has already been consumed by the standard function parsing logic.

Let me look at how the standard function parsing works. In `_parse_function_call`, the flow is:

1. Parse function name and opening paren (automatically by the caller)
2. Parse arguments using `_parse_csv` 
3. Match closing parenthesis with `self._match_r_paren(this)`
4. Call `self._parse_window(this)` 

But in my custom parser, I'm doing:
1. Manually check for opening paren
2. Parse arguments 
3. Manually check for closing paren
4. Call `self._parse_window`

The issue is that `_parse_window` expects the closing paren to NOT have been consumed yet, but I'm consuming it manually.

The conversion pattern is:
- `Dot(Dot(Identifier(DB), Identifier(SCHEMA)), Anonymous(FUNC))` 
- Becomes `Column(Window(Anonymous(FUNC)), table=SCHEMA, db=DB)`

This suggests that during window parsing, the parser recognizes that this is a qualified reference to a function and converts it to a Column with db/table qualifiers.

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Found it! Lines 5129-5137 show exactly where the conversion from Dot to Column happens!

When we have a Column (like `x.y`) and we're applying a dot operation with a field (like `z`), if the Column doesn't have a catalog, it gets converted to a new Column with:
- `this=field` (the z part)  
- `table=this.this` (the y part)
- `db=this.args.get("table")` (the x part)

This is the logic that converts `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` from a Dot structure to a Column with db/table qualifiers.

But this is happening in the context of column operations parsing, not in window parsing directly.

The flow is:
1. Parse `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()` as `Dot(Dot(Identifier(DB), Identifier(SCHEMA)), Anonymous(FUNC))`
2. When `OVER` is encountered, `_parse_window` is called on this Dot expression
3. Somewhere in the process (likely in column ops parsing), the Dot gets converted to a Column
4. Then `_parse_window` operates on the Column

Now I understand the issue better. The problem is that when parsing expressions that might become window functions, Dot expressions representing qualified function calls get converted to Columns prematurely.

The fix should be to prevent this conversion when we're dealing with expressions that might be function calls with window clauses.

But actually, let me double-check this theory by looking at where `_parse_window` gets called and what expression it receives.

Looking back at the debug trace, when I parse `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()`, the result is a Column, which means the conversion happened before `_parse_window` was even called.

So the real issue is in `_parse_column_ops` or related parsing logic that converts Dot expressions to Columns when they shouldn't be converted if they represent function calls.

Let me check if there's a way to detect in the column ops parsing that we're dealing with a function call rather than a column reference.

Looking at lines 5129-5137 again, the condition is `isinstance(this, exp.Column) and not this.args.get("catalog")`. This condition doesn't distinguish between column references and function calls.

But the real issue might be earlier. Let me trace the exact parsing flow for `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()`:

1. `_parse_column_reference()` is called
2. This calls `_parse_field()` 
3. This eventually leads to `_parse_column_ops()` when dots are encountered
4. In `_parse_column_ops`, `DB.SCHEMA` gets parsed as a Column
5. Then `.FUNC()` gets parsed as a field operation
6. The logic at lines 5129-5137 converts the Column to a new Column with FUNC as the `this` field
7. But FUNC is an Anonymous function, not an identifier

Wait, let me check what exactly gets parsed as the field in step 5. When we have `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC()`, the `FUNC()` part should be parsed as an Anonymous function.

But the logic at lines 5129-5137 assumes that `field` is an identifier-like thing to become the `this` of a Column.

If `field` is actually an Anonymous function, then creating a Column with `this=field` where field is a function would create the structure we see: `Column(this=Window(Anonymous(FUNC)), table=..., db=...)`.

This makes sense! The issue is that when parsing `DB.SCHEMA.FUNC() OVER ()`:

1. `DB.SCHEMA` gets parsed as `Column(this=SCHEMA, table=DB)`  
2. `.FUNC()` gets parsed as a field operation where `field` is `Anonymous(FUNC)`
3. The column ops logic converts this to `Column(this=Anonymous(FUNC), table=SCHEMA, db=DB)`
4. Then `_parse_window` wraps the Anonymous(FUNC) in a Window

So the fix should be to detect when `field` is a function and handle that case differently.
